5a:["$","p",null,{"children":"This tutorial uses PEC with TREX directly, so that you can complete it without an AI agent.","className":"group paragraph-el text-text-primary mx-0"}]
5b:["$","p",null,{"children":"PEC is a technique that mitigates quantum noise when you calculate the expectation values of observables. Under ideal conditions, PEC can produce an unbiased estimate of the expectation values. It learns the noise of each layer of two-qubit gates, then runs an ensemble of circuits that, on average, apply the inverse of that noise. PEC uses Pauli twirling to tailor the noise into a Pauli channel, which can be learned and inverted efficiently.","className":"group paragraph-el text-text-primary mx-0"}]
5c:["$","p",null,{"children":"In practice, PEC is only as accurate as the learned noise model. The sparse Pauli-Lindblad model includes only one- and two-qubit error terms on neighboring qubits, and it cannot fully capture effects such as crosstalk between distant qubits or time-correlated errors. The device noise can also drift between noise learning and execution. PEC does not cancel noise that the model misses, and it overcompensates for noise that the model overestimates. Therefore, mitigated results can still deviate from the ideal values in either direction.","className":"group paragraph-el text-text-primary mx-0"}]
5d:["$","p",null,{"children":"TREX is a technique that mitigates measurement errors, and is useful when you estimate the expectation values of Pauli observables. It relies on measurement twirling, which diagonalizes the readout-error transfer matrix and makes it easy to invert. TREX learns a rescaling term from the diagonal noise channel by measuring twirled calibration circuits that are initialized in the zero state.","className":"group paragraph-el text-text-primary mx-0"}]

Instead, it groups them into chunks and runs one chunk per shot loop. d5:["$","p",null,{"children":"All jobs that are submitted to a real QPU require some classical pre-processing, such as translation into pulses that can be sent to the hardware. Because this pre-processing can be done in parallel, the service dequeues and starts multiple jobs at a time. The QPU, however, can process only one unit of work at a time. Therefore, if your job generates multiple chunks, they might be interleaved with the chunks of other users' jobs, and each chunk might have to wait for its turn on the QPU.","className":"group paragraph-el text-text-primary mx-0"}]
